# Daily Papers
The project automatically fetches the latest papers from arXiv based on keywords.

The subheadings in the README file represent the search keywords.

Only the most recent articles for each keyword are retained, up to a maximum of 100 papers.

You can click the 'Watch' button to receive daily email notifications.

Last update: 2026-11-02

## Multimodal Learning
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[Fusion or Confusion? Multimodal Complexity Is Not All You Need](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.22991v4)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal learning has become a prominent research area, with the potential of substantial performance gains by combining information across modalities. At the same time, model development has trended toward increasingly complex deep learning architectures, motivated by the assumption that multimodal-specific methods improve performance. We challenge this assumption through a large-scale empirical study by reimplementing 19 high-impact multimodal methods across nine diverse datasets with up to 23 modalities. Under standardized experimental conditions, including hyperparameter tuning, weight initialization, cross-validation, and statistical testing, increased multimodal complexity often yields confusion rather than effective fusion of data modalities. Accordingly, complex multimodal architectures do not reliably outperform unimodal baselines and a Simple Baseline for Multimodal Learning (SimBaMM). Through a focused case study, we further demonstrate concrete methodological shortcomings even in top-tier multimodal learning publications, underscoring the need for standardized evaluation practices. In summary, we argue for a shift in focus for multimodal learning: away from the pursuit of architectural novelty and toward methodological rigor.</p></details> |  |
| **[Soil Moisture Estimation in Precision Agriculture: A Knowledge Distillation Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40432v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Accurate soil moisture estimation is critical for precision agriculture, influencing irrigation, fertilization, and harvesting strategies. Traditional methods primarily rely on meteorological and soil property data, while recent approaches incorporate visual modalities such as aerial and geospatial imagery. However, these high-resolution images are expensive and computationally demanding to analyze. In this work, we propose Soil Moisture Distillation (SMD), a Knowledge Distillation (KD)-based multimodal learning framework that leverages unimodal teachers to enhance multimodal soil moisture estimation. SMD surpasses not only traditional multimodal fusion techniques, including MIS-ME, but also outperforms unimodal models, demonstrating the benefits of guided unimodal knowledge transfer. Additionally, we conduct a comparative analysis of soil patches and crop images, showing that crop images provide superior visual cues, leading to improved prediction accuracy. SMD achieves a 2% improvement in MAPE for soil patches and 1.2% for crops over state-of-the-art baselines, highlighting the effectiveness of leveraging both meteorological and visual data through knowledge distillation for enhanced soil moisture forecasting.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by MWAIS 2025</p></details> |
| **[IMPACT: Inverter-Informed Multimodal Learning for Photovoltaic Generation Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40418v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This work develops a physics-informed multimodal deep learning framework for 10- to 110-minute-ahead probabilistic photovoltaic power forecasting. The forecasting model is built on a four-branch architecture that separately encodes plant-level temporal history and inverter-level spatial cloud dynamics through a graph attention encoder with a learned adjacency, known-future solar geometry, and the current meteorological state, fused through gated cross-attention. The geometry branch adds two forward-looking physical signals, spatial cloud heterogeneity and inverter ramp-down activity, each empirically weighted toward the horizon where it is most predictive. The decoder anchors its prediction to a smart persistence baseline, learning a horizon-conditioned correction, and the resulting quantile forecasts are calibrated online through a kernel-weighted adaptive conformal calibration step. The proposed model outperforms all 14 baselines, achieving a forecast skill score (FSS) of 0.2950, 0.099 above the next-best model. A branch-composition ablation shows that every branch contributes positively and the model retains substantial skill in challenging overcast and variable sky conditions (FSS 0.198 and 0.255).</p></details> |  |
| **[Tackling Multimodal Learning Challenges with Mixture-of-Expert: A Survey](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27431v2)** | 2026-10-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) presents a naturally compatible and scalable framework for multimodal learning, demonstrating strong adaptability across diverse modalities and tasks. Despite its growing success, a comprehensive and systematic review on the MoE metho addressing multimodal challenges remains lacking. Existing surveys tend to evaluate either multimodal learning or MoE independently from method taxonomy, overlooking the unique interplay between them. This survey fills that gap by answering a central question: \textit{How does MoE effectively resolve multimodal challenges?} We approach this from three key perspectives: (1) \textbf{MoE as an Efficient Multimodal Engine:} enabling scalable multimodal modeling by decoupling computational cost from parameter growth and mitigating modality redundancy through selective expert activation; (2) \textbf{MoE as a Multimodal Representation Learner:} integrating complementary multi-opinion expert knowledge to enrich alignment and interaction representations; and (3) \textbf{MoE as a Multimodal Adapter:} providing a modular and flexible mechanism to model imperfect data scenarios such as modality imbalance and missing modality. Through our extensive literature review, we identify critical research gaps, including interpretable routing, expert communication, modality integration, and lifelong multimodal learning. We position this survey as a foundation for future research toward interpretable and sustainable multimodal Mixture-of-Experts system.</p></details> | <details><summary>This ...</summary><p>This survey has been accepted by IJCAI 2026. Results were released by 30 April 2026. This latest replacement is an extension version of survey to the previous version, which has been submitted to ACM Computing Survey for peer-review</p></details> |
| **[Grounding Long Financial Text with Semantically-Enriched Tables via Multimodal Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37913v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We propose a multimodal transformer framework for efficiently modeling long1 financial documents that combine structured tabular data with noisy, unstructured2 textual disclosures. The framework represents financial tables as semantically3 enriched token sequences and integrates them with long-form text through a cross-4 attention mechanism that explicitly captures cross-modal dependencies. In par-5 ticular, we augment each numerical variable with a semantic embedding derived6 from its textual definition, enabling the model to encode both numerical magnitude7 and economic meaning. This design yields a more expressive and parameter-8 efficient representation of tabular data compared to value-only embeddings. Using9 a large-scale dataset of financial statements and MD&A disclosures, we show that10 structured numerical features remain the dominant source of predictive signal;11 however, textual information provides consistent incremental gains when incor-12 porated via cross-attention rather than simple concatenation. Our results further13 demonstrate that noisy textual disclosures are most useful when conditioned on a14 stable, semantically structured tabular representation. In particular, using frozen15 variable-level tabular representations as cross-attention queries enables the model16 to weight MD&A chunks differently for each financial variable and observation.17 These findings suggest that cross-attention provides an efficient mechanism for18 table-conditioned aggregation of long-form narrative evidence.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to Neurips Long-context foundation model workshop</p></details> |
| **[Task-Centric Multimodal Learning for Cognitive Impairment Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20906v2)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Dementia affects millions of older adults worldwide, yet early detection remains difficult outside clinical settings. Traditional models primarily rely on text or voice-based responses, capturing only a limited view of human cognition despite the inherently multimodal nature of human expression. This work proposes a contactless, multimodal screening framework that leverages video, audio, and text signals collected during structured voice-based interactions with Amazon Alexa. By incorporating these modalities, our approach captures a more holistic representation of human behavior encompassing speech patterns, vocal characteristics, facial expressions, and linguistic structure, which may enable more accurate detection of cognitive decline. Different participants completed three conversational tasks designed to probe spontaneous speech, comprehension, and recall. We pursued three objectives: (1) evaluate which of the three Alexa tasks provides the most informative cognitive signals, (2) compare the effectiveness of unimodal models, and (3) assess the benefits of multimodal fusion models. Task-level analysis shows that the video model achieves its best performance on Command-Reading, with an accuracy of 0.65 (AUC 0.69), while the audio model performs best on Question-Answering, reaching an accuracy of 0.67 (AUC 0.73). In contrast, the multimodal model demonstrates consistently stable performance across tasks, achieving an accuracy of 0.76 (AUC 0.75) on Command-Reading and 0.76 (AUC 0.73) on Question-Answering. These findings demonstrate that multimodal behavioral cues captured through naturalistic interactions can reliably distinguish MCI from healthy aging, highlighting the limitations of traditional diagnostic paradigms and supporting the shift toward multimodal, real-world cognitive assessment frameworks.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted for publication at the 2026 IEEE Global Communications Conference (GLOBECOM)</p></details> |
| **[MultiFair: Multimodal Balanced Fairness-Aware Medical Classification with Dual-Level Gradient Modulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.07328v3)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Medical decision systems increasingly rely on data from multiple sources to ensure reliable and unbiased diagnosis. However, existing multimodal learning models fail to achieve this goal because they often overlook two critical challenges. First, various data modalities may learn unevenly, thereby converging to a model biased towards certain modalities. Second, the model may emphasize learning on certain demographic groups causing unfair performances. The two aspects can influence each other, as different data modalities may favor respective groups during optimization, leading to both imbalanced and unfair multimodal learning. This paper proposes a novel approach called MultiFair for multimodal medical classification, which addresses these challenges with a dual-level gradient modulation process. MultiFair dynamically modulates training gradients regarding the optimization direction and magnitude at both data modality and group levels. We evaluate MultiFair on three real-world medical classification datasets with diverse demographic attributes,including multiclass classification and missing-modality settings. Experimental results demonstrate its effectiveness.</p></details> | <details><summary>This ...</summary><p>This work has been accepted for publication in IEEE Transactions on Medical Imaging</p></details> |
| **[Peers4U: Designing Learner-Centered Virtual Peers for Children's Multimodal Mathematics Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37238v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In early mathematics, children learn abstract concepts through concrete representations, such as manipulating objects, drawing, and speaking. Learning with peers can enrich this process by exposing children to diverse strategies and enabling them to exchange ideas through interaction. We realized this potential with virtual peers in Peers4U, a multimodal learning system to enhance children's mathematical conceptual understanding. Virtual peers driven by LLMs are personalized to the learner's cognitive level and assigned multimodal solutions with inspectable processes. During learning, peers engage in mixed-initiative interactions by responding, initiating topics, and advancing discussions along a pedagogical progression. A user study with 22 children and two experts showed our learner-centered topic orchestration significantly outperformed a persona-centered baseline, and children spontaneously authored new artifacts to demonstrate inspired ideas or share their own strategies, producing significantly more solutions when grouped with more Leading Peers. We contribute design insights for multimodal, learner-centered virtual peer systems.</p></details> |  |
| **[ML-ECS: Heterogeneity-Aware Collaborative Multimodal Learning for Edge-Cloud Synergies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.14107v2)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Edge-cloud synergies provide a promising paradigm for privacy-preserving deployment of foundation models in heterogeneous web environments, enabling lightweight edge models to support domain-specific multimodal we b services while cloud-hosted models facilitate collaborative knowledge sharing. However, in practical edge-cloud web environments, collaborative multimodal learning is challenged by modality heterogeneity (different modality combinations across domains) and model-structure heterogeneity (different modality-specific encoders/fusion modules). To address these issues, we propose ML-ECS, a collaborative multimodal learning framework that enables joint training between a server-based model and heterogeneous edge models. This framework consists of four components: (1) cross-modal contrastive learning (CCL) to align modality representations in a shared latent space, (2) adaptive multimodal tuning (AMT) to preserve domain-specific knowledge from local datasets, (3) modality-aware aggregation (MAA) to robustly aggregate while mitigating noise caused by missing modalities, and (4) SLM-enhanced CCL (SE-CCL) to facilitate bidirectional knowledge transfer between cloud and edge. Experimental results on various multimodal tasks show that ML-ECS consistently outperform state-of-the-art baselines under varying modality availability, achieving improvements of 1.55% to 16.75% in Rouge-LSum and improving both client- and server-side performance. In addition, by communicating only low-rank LoRA parameters and fused representations, ML-ECS achieves high communication efficiency, requiring only 0.71% of the total parameter volume.</p></details> |  |
| **[VaViT: Vanilla ViT for Automotive Point Cloud Semantic Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.31177v2)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Plain Transformers have become the de-facto architecture for processing text, audio, image, and video, offering a unified backbone for multimodal learning. However, state-of-the-art architectures for point cloud semantic segmentation remain dominated by U-Nets architectures where convolutions are interleaved with local or windowed attentions. In this work, we show how to effectively leverage vanilla, non-hierarchical ViTs for the segmentation of large-scale automotive lidar scenes. We bridge the performance gap thanks to a carefully designed tokenizer, a lightweight decoder segmentation head, and tailored data augmentations. Our approach, VaViT, for Vanilla ViT, matches or exceeds the performance of state-of-the-art methods while maintaining the simplicity of ViT architecture. We provide extensive evaluations on nuScenes, SemanticKITTI, and Waymo Open Dataset to validate the efficiency of our method. Code and pre-trained models are available at https://github.com/valeoai/VaViT.</p></details> | WACV 2027 |
| **[Ampe-DB: A Dataset for Human Activity and Interaction Analysis Using the Ghanaian Game Ampe](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33130v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Human activity recognition benchmarks underrepresent culturally situated forms of movement and interaction. We introduce Ampe-DB, a multimodal dataset based on Ampe, a traditional Ghanaian game involving coordinated jumping, leg movements, and two-person interaction. It contains 637 canonical clips from 15 participants, with RGB video, two-person pose representations, participant and pair metadata, four-class movement labels, and derived binary movement-relation labels. We evaluate four-class movement recognition and binary movement-relation classification using three protocols: a duplicate-safe fixed benchmark, four-fold pair-held-out evaluation, and participant-held-out diagnostics for participants with complete class coverage. Baselines comprise a frozen Kinetics-400-pretrained R(2+1)D-18 RGB encoder, a two-layer bidirectional GRU for pose sequences, and validation-selected RGB-pose late fusion. For four-class recognition, RGB macro-F1 falls from 0.955 on the fixed benchmark to 0.418 under pair-held-out evaluation and 0.384 under participant-held-out evaluation. Pose is more robust, achieving 0.957, 0.811, and 0.839 across the same protocols. Fusion reaches 0.976 on the fixed benchmark and 0.824 under pair-held-out evaluation, but does not consistently improve on pose under participant shift. Binary movement-relation recognition also becomes substantially harder under held-out evaluation, especially for different-leg configurations. These findings show that strong fixed-benchmark scores can mask failures to generalize across people and pairings. Ampe-DB supports research on fine-grained movement recognition, human-human interaction, multimodal learning, and identity-aware generalization.</p></details> | <details><summary>74 pa...</summary><p>74 pages, 9 figures, 7 numbered tables. Ampe-DB V1 dataset (637 interaction clips) available at https://huggingface.co/datasets/RuthBiney/Ampe-DB</p></details> |
| **[Reliability-Aware Probabilistic Fusion for Multimodal Learning with Incomplete Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29621v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal inputs may be missing or degraded, raising distinct questions of evidence availability and trust. We introduce a reliability-aware Gaussian Product-of-Experts (PoE) in which an input-dependent reliability weight tempers each modality's latent precision, while missing experts are omitted. We characterize how reliability and availability affect posterior information and expert influence. Across two clinical multimodal tasks, the full framework remains competitive with complete inputs and achieves its clearest mean-AUROC advantage under modality absence, particularly on HAIM-MIMIC-MM. Ablations show a substantial gain from missing-aware training and an additional HAIM improvement from reliability weighting under matched PoE, missing-aware, and consistency training. Unseen test-time corruptions are used diagnostically and reveal modality-dependent reliability-weight and latent-uncertainty responses, including failure under severe degradation. Thus, reliability weighting provides an explicit evidence-control mechanism, while learning dependable quality signals remains an open challenge.</p></details> |  |
| **[On the Complementarity of Quantum and Classical Features: Adaptive Hybrid Quantum-Classical Feature Fusion for Breast Cancer Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.22903v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The integration of quantum machine learning with classical deep learning offers promising avenues for medical image analysis by mapping data into high-dimensional Hilbert spaces. However, unifying these paradigms remains challenging due to optimization asymmetries. We propose a hybrid quantum-classical architecture for breast cancer diagnosis based on a dual-branch feature-extraction pipeline. Our framework unifies complementary representations from classical models and trainable or deterministic (non-trainable) quantum circuits. Three fusion strategies are introduced: Static Hybrid Fusion (SHF) for offline extraction, Dynamic Hybrid Fusion (DHF) for end-to-end co-adaptation, and Temperature-Scaled Hybrid Fusion (TSHF). Inspired by multimodal learning, TSHF uses a learnable scalar to balance branch gradient dynamics and address optimization bottlenecks. We evaluate BreastMNIST, INbreast, and BUS-UCLM, spanning ultrasound and mammography, using ResNet-18 and a shallow CNN, with five independent runs per configuration. Fusion improves over the strongest classical baseline on all three benchmarks. On BreastMNIST, TSHF with ResNet-18 and a non-trainable circuit achieves 88.33% accuracy, 92.09% F1-score, and 92.09% AUC-ROC, versus 84.74% accuracy and 87.08% AUC-ROC for ResNet-18 alone. On INbreast, SHF with the same pairing performs best, at 70.42% accuracy, 72.96% F1-score, and 80.28% AUC-ROC. On BUS-UCLM, SHF with a trainable circuit achieves 78.46% accuracy and 84.66% F1-score, over seven accuracy points above the best standalone branch. Adaptive fusion is not universally beneficial: static fusion performs best on the two smaller datasets, suggesting an additional estimation burden from co-adaptation and learned calibration under limited data. Clinical applicability requires validation beyond the small, low-resolution datasets and simulated quantum circuits considered here.</p></details> | <details><summary>41 pa...</summary><p>41 pages, 16 figures. This manuscript is a preprint under review at Artificial Intelligence in Medicine</p></details> |
| **[Beyond Invariance: Factor-wise Selective Treatment of Shifts for Multimodal Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21403v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Distribution shifts pose a fundamental challenge to robust multimodal learning. Existing methods typically promote invariance by suppressing environment-dependent variations, implicitly applying a uniform treatment to shifted information. However, shifted information can have different predictive consequences: some contributes little to prediction, some remains reliably informative, and some exhibits environment-dependent predictive effects. This raises a fundamental question: which shifted information should be suppressed, preserved, or attenuated? To address this question, we propose FaShift, a factor-wise shift framework that diagnoses latent representation factors according to their distributional changes, intervention effects, and cross-environment stability. By factorizing multimodal representations and evaluating these complementary properties, FaShift distinguishes non-predictive factors, stably predictive factors, and predictively useful but unstable factors. It then selectively suppresses, preserves, or attenuates their contributions, rather than enforcing uniform invariance. Experiments on MOSI, MOSEI, and CH-SIMS show that FaShift achieves the best ID accuracy and F1 across all three datasets. Compared with the strongest competitors reporting OOD results, it improves OOD accuracy/F1 by 1.5/3.6 points on MOSI and 1.7/2.7 points on MOSEI, while achieving the best reported OOD accuracy and F1 on CH-SIMS.</p></details> |  |
| **[Reusing Multimodal Agents: Efficient Evaluation of Sound Designs in Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27670v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This paper introduces a novel framework for efficiently evaluating in-game sound designs using multimodal learning and reinforcement learning. Sound design significantly influences gameplay and accessibility; however, objective evaluation methods, defined here as automated assessments based on agent performance rather than human ratings, often require retraining agents from scratch for each sound design. Our approach first pre-trains a multimodal encoder that encodes visual and auditory information into a shared latent space. A reinforcement learning agent is then trained using this latent representation. We fine-tune the multimodal encoder for each sound design while keeping the trained policy fixed, allowing us to isolate the effects of sound changes on agent behavior. To evaluate the proposed method, we conducted experiments on games from multiple genres, comparing the performance of blind agents (agents using only sound input) under various sound designs. Experimental results suggest that this approach can detect differences in agent performance caused by degraded or altered sound design. Moreover, we significantly reduce the computational cost compared to retraining the agent from scratch. Experimental results suggest that this approach provides a practical and scalable solution for evaluating in-game sound designs. The source code, data and supplementary document are available: https://github.com/thainv0212/tog-reusing-agents.</p></details> |  |
| **[VLX-Go: Robust Embodied Tracking through Multi-Stage Learning and On-Policy Refinement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19361v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Embodied visual tracking requires a robot to identify a designated target from visual observations and control its own motion to follow that target through an environment. Following an isolated person is a comparatively simple demonstration of embodied visual tracking, but following the intended person among distractors remains challenging. Existing methods exhibit a substantial drop in success from single-target to distractor tracking, motivating a focus on the shared spaces that person-following robots must navigate. We present VLX-GO, a vision-language waypoint policy built around the hypothesis that visual and language representations should be aligned before tracking-specific motion is learned. The policy combines complementary frozen visual encoders, multiscale temporal context, a transformer backbone, and a lightweight head for robot-centric waypoint prediction. Its training progresses through vision--language alignment, multimodal reasoning mid-training, and tracking specialization on 19,532 oracle episodes. Compared with 41.54% distractor-tracking success for our earlier OmTrackVLA model, the complete multi-stage recipe achieves 55.80%. We then explore on-policy refinement using filtered oracle labels for states visited by the policy. This preliminary study adds 2.42 percentage points of distractor-tracking success, reaching 58.22%, while reducing collision rate by 1.99 pts. The final policy records the highest distractor- and ambiguous-tracking success rates among the compared methods. These results support staged multimodal learning as a promising direction for tracking among distractors and motivate further study of on-policy refinement for person following in shared spaces. Code is available at https://github.com/om-ai-lab/VLX-Go.</p></details> | tech report |
| **[CheXFuse: Guided Differential Joint Attention Fusion for Robust Multimodal Chest X-Ray Classification in Data-Scarce Medical Settings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25617v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A central challenge in multimodal learning is how to fuse modalities that differ substantially in predictive strength. When one modality provides a much stronger representation than another, indiscriminate cross-modal interaction can introduce weak, redundant, or unstable signals, limiting or even degrading performance relative to the strongest unimodal representation. To address this problem, we propose CheXFuse, a multimodal fusion framework based on Guided Differential Joint Attention (GuDJA). CheXFuse combines image and text token representations within a joint multimodal sequence, where learned token guidance emphasizes informative representations and differential attention reduces less useful attention responses. We evaluate CheXFuse on the Indiana University (IU) Chest X-ray dataset, using binary (normal vs. abnormal) classification as the downstream task. Experiments are conducted under both original-report and masked-report settings, with the latter serving as a controlled stress test under reduced textual informativeness. CheXFuse with GuDJA achieves the strongest mean overall performance across the evaluated baselines and ablations, reaching 95.73% accuracy, 95.79% F1-score, 96.54% recall, and 95.28% sensitivity at 95% specificity (Sens@95Spec) with original reports, and 95.58%, 95.60%, 95.60%, and 94.34%, respectively, under masked reports. These results suggest that GuDJA provides a promising fusion strategy under asymmetric or partially reduced modality informativeness.</p></details> | IEEE ICDM DMBIH 2026 |
| **[MM-LeJEPA: Correlated Gaussian Regularization for Multimodal Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24681v1)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LeJEPA derives an isotropic Gaussian embedding target by minimizing, under its task assumptions, a Fisher information criterion that controls the task-averaged squared prediction bias. This enables unimodal self-supervised learning without reconstruction decoders, exponential-moving-average teachers, or contrastive negatives. However, its isotropic task prior does not account for the cross-modal structure of multimodal tasks that read shared content. We introduce MM-LeJEPA by extending LeJEPA's task assumptions to multimodal learning. Specifically, we model multimodal tasks as strength-weighted combinations of components that read independent information from the two modalities and components that read the same content from both. Under explicit gradient-moment, correspondence, and symmetry assumptions, we derive a correlated Gaussian target that minimizes a weighted Fisher information functional under a fixed total variance constraint. An analytic transform adapts Sketched Isotropic Gaussian Regularization (SIGReg) to this target, yielding a two-term objective that combines distributional regularization with local-to-global prediction. The extension adds a single scalar hyperparameter, the target cross-modal correlation, and retains the simplicity of LeJEPA. On public audio-visual classification benchmarks, our results are competitive with representative open-source methods. Code will be released.</p></details> |  |
| **[AdamPM: Memory-Efficient LLM Optimization via Moment Pooling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24598v1)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Adam is widely used for training large language models (LLMs) owing to its strong optimization performance and training stability, but its element-wise estimation for the first and second moments incur substantial memory overhead. Although numerous memory-efficient optimizers have been proposed, many incur additional computational overhead or require additional tuning of hyperparameters. Consequently, Adam remains a dominant and reliable choice for LLM pre-training. We propose AdamPM (Adam with Pooled Moments), a simple and memory-efficient variant of Adam that groups adjacent moment entries along the row or column dimension and represents each group by a single shared mean. AdamPM can well inherit Adam's hyperparameter settings and is readily integrated into existing Adam optimizer implementations, with less memory overhead. We systematically investigate pooling of both first and second moment estimates and identify second moment pooling as the effective design. With a group size of $s$, AdamPM reduces second moment memory to $1/s$ of the original requirement. Extensive experiments on language model pretraining, fine-tuning and multimodal learning demonstrate that AdamPM maintains model performance and training speed while substantially reducing optimizer-state memory. Our code is available at \url{https://github.com/jixiaowei95/AdamPM}.</p></details> | 19 pages |
| **[MSCR: Jointly Balancing Utilization Across Modalities and Discovering Synergistic Information](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24395v1)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In multimodal learning, modality imbalance often lets one modality dominate optimization and keeps the model from fully exploiting cross-modal interactions. Balance-oriented methods such as the Multimodal Competition Regularizer (MCR) perform well by alleviating this imbalance during training. These dynamic balancing methods, however, mainly adjust modality contributions and do not consider how imbalance affects the formation of synergistic information during fusion. Synergy is a major source of multimodal advantage, because it is predictive gain that cannot be recovered from unimodal evidence alone. We propose the Multimodal Synergistic Competition Regularizer (MSCR), a fusion method that jointly promotes balanced modality utilization and the discovery of synergistic information. MSCR has two complementary objectives. An anti-dominance objective suppresses excessive unimodal influence and leaves room for synergistic information, and a synergy objective encourages the fused predictor to outperform a non-synergistic unimodal reference. An adaptive gate further concentrates the regularization on samples that need stronger collaborative modeling. Experiments on five benchmarks show that MSCR consistently improves performance over MCR and strengthens synergistic capability. It raises conditional recovery, the accuracy of the fused prediction on samples that the unimodal predictors misclassify, as well as the share of the model's output that InterSHAP attributes to cross-modal interactions. Its gain in conditional recovery persists when the fusion module is Transformer-based. Code is available at https://github.com/chenxinyu991-collab/MSCR.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[Cattle-CLIP: A Multimodal Framework for Dairy Cattle Behaviour Recognition from Video](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.09203v3)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Monitoring cattle behaviour provides valuable insights into animal health, productivity and welfare. However, robust behaviour recognition in real-world farm environments remains challenging because well-annotated livestock video datasets are scarce and agricultural surveillance footage differs substantially from large-scale pretraining data. To address these challenges, we propose Cattle-CLIP, a domain-adaptive vision-language framework based on Contrastive Language-Image Pretraining (CLIP), which reformulates cattle behaviour recognition as a cross-modal semantic alignment task. Cattle-CLIP incorporates a temporal integration module that extends image-level contrastive pretraining to video-based behaviour understanding and promotes consistent semantic alignment across time. To reduce domain discrepancy, customised data augmentation and behaviour-oriented prompts are employed. We also construct CattleBehaviours6, a curated dataset containing 1,905 annotated clips from six indoor behaviours: feeding, drinking, standing-self-grooming, standing-ruminating, lying-self-grooming and lying-ruminating. The dataset provides standardised ethogram definitions for model training, evaluation and future livestock behaviour research. Cattle-CLIP was evaluated under supervised and few-shot learning settings, with particular emphasis on data-constrained behaviour recognition. In supervised experiments, the model achieved 96.1% accuracy across six behaviours and showed high precision and recall for feeding, drinking and standing-ruminating. It also maintained promising few-shot performance with limited labelled data, demonstrating the potential of multimodal learning for cattle behaviour recognition in data-constrained farm environments.</p></details> |  |
| **[Pathway-Structured Privileged Distillation for Deployable Computational Pathology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02877v3)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Integrating transcriptomics and histopathology can improve cancer risk modelling, yet practical use is constrained by the limited availability of RNA profiling in routine settings. Here we introduce Mixture of Pathway Experts (MoPE), a knowledge-distillation framework that reframes multimodal learning as privileged distillation for histology-only inference. MoPE is motivated by the partial observability between RNA profiles and whole-slide images: histology can capture morphology-linked consequences of certain molecular programmes, but cannot be expected to reconstruct the full transcriptomic state. MoPE encodes RNA-derived pathways and transfers the molecular supervision to pathway-indexed pathology experts through memory-usage alignment. Across diverse public benchmarks and two independent breast cancer cohorts, MoPE consistently improved WSI-only inference performance relative to baseline methods. Pathway-usage analyses and human-audited visual inspection provide bounded inspection of model behaviour and candidate morphology-linked readouts. According to our literature search, the proposed method achieves state-of-the-art (SOTA) accuracy of 93.29% on the UPMC Food-101 dataset, while we apply the ViT-L/16 model for vision and the GPT-2 model for text. We share the scripts of the proposed method with traditional counterparts at the following repository: github.com/dipuk0506/multimodal</p></details> |  |
| **[Improving Fake Profile Detection Through Multimodal Learning and Research-Guided Feature Integration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23926v1)** | 2026-10-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Fake accounts continue to undermine online platforms, yet it remains unclear whether multimodal learning offers meaningful improvements over strong metadata baselines or graph-based approaches. | **[Adaptive Hierarchical Representation Alliance for Multimodal Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22863v3)** | 2026-09-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal models often align language, vision, and audio in a single final-layer latent space, implicitly assuming that task-relevant evidence emerges at the same semantic depth across modalities. | **[From Models to Systems: A Comprehensive Survey of Efficient Multimodal Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19445v1)** | 2026-09-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The rapid expansion of multimodal models has surfaced formidable bottlenecks in computation, memory, and deployment, catalyzing the rise of Efficient Multimodal Learning (EML) as a pivotal research frontier. Through an integrative case study of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs), we trace the field's evolutionary trajectory from initial structural adjustments to modern full-stack resource orchestration. Furthermore, we provide a holistic discussion and application-specific optimization blueprints for diverse domains and posit a paradigm shift toward self-regulating intelligence, where efficiency is an intrinsic, emergent property of the model's fundamental design rather than a post-hoc constraint. Finally, we present open challenges and future directions that will define the trajectory of EML research. This survey establishes a structured framework for multimodal systems that are not only high-performing and generalizable but natively efficient and ready for ubiquitous deployment. We review 170 recent studies (2018--2026) and 12 foundational works, organizing multimodal learning around four challenges: cross-modal alignment, information-efficient fusion, interaction-type characterization, and scaling to multimodal foundation models. We further discuss how emerging multimodal foundation models instantiate these principles at scale and identify open challenges including scalable information estimation in high-dimensional settings, standardized evaluation across information-theoretic methods, combinatorial complexity of multimodal PID, and the transition from post-hoc information analysis toward information-aware multimodal learning.</p></details> |  |

| **[Semantics at an Angle: When Cosine Similarity Works Until It Doesn't](https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.16318v4)** | 2026-08-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Cosine similarity is a standard comparison rule for learned representations in information retrieval, natural language processing, computer vision, and multimodal learning. These same properties also delimit what cosine can express. Normalization discards radial variation; anisotropic representations can compress angular contrast; high-dimensional neighborhoods can develop hubs; and a symmetric, uncalibrated score may be mismatched to the relation of interest. This article offers a selective review organized around a simple principle: the usefulness of cosine similarity depends jointly on the learned representation, any normalization or post-processing, the scoring rule, and the downstream decision. We derive the main geometric identities, distinguish failure mechanisms that are often conflated, review representative evidence about embedding norms, and describe objective-matched, geometry-aware, hubness-aware, norm-aware, and learned alternatives. The central conclusion is conditional rather than adversarial: cosine's positive-scale invariance is justified when radial variation is nuisance or fixed by the representation contract, but its angular geometry and downstream decision must still be validated.</p></details> |  |

In the Adaptive Training stage, a GMM-guided Adaptive Loss dynamically reallocates optimization priorities, imposing stronger modality alignment penalties on imbalanced samples while prioritizing multimodal fusion for balanced ones. | **[Quantum Models with Multi-Stage Training for Compositional Concept Generalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15601v1)** | 2026-08-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Compositional Concept Generalization (CoCoGen), the ability to systematically recombine learned primitives in novel contexts, is a key challenge for multimodal learning. ## Representation Learning
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[Variational decomposition autoencoding improves disentanglement of latent representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.06844v2)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Understanding the structure of complex, nonstationary, high-dimensional time-evolving signals is a central challenge in scientific data analysis. In many domains, such as speech and biomedical signal processing, the ability to learn disentangled and interpretable representations is critical for uncovering latent generative mechanisms. Traditional approaches to unsupervised representation learning, including variational autoencoders (VAEs), often struggle to capture the temporal and spectral diversity inherent in such data. Here we introduce variational decomposition autoencoding (VDA), a framework that incorporates a strong structural bias toward signal decomposition into variational representation learning. VDA is instantiated through variational decomposition autoencoders (DecVAEs), i.e., encoder-only neural networks that perform latent autoencoding at the level of distributions to learn multiple latent subspaces aligned with time-frequency characteristics. We evaluate DecVAEs on simulated data and three publicly available scientific datasets, spanning speech recognition, dysarthric speech evaluation, and emotional speech classification. DecVAEs improve on VAE-based methods in disentanglement quality and robustness, and their latent subspaces become factor-specific when the generative factors have distinct spectral signatures. These findings position decomposition-aware architectures as potential tools for extracting structured representations from speech, and potentially from other dynamic signals with factors exhibiting distinct spectral signatures, with applications in clinical diagnostics, human-computer interaction, and adaptive neurotechnologies.</p></details> | <details><summary>Suppl...</summary><p>Supplementary information file at: https://drive.google.com/drive/folders/1sZl2AcCtRK-1oav7XZSaxlu0Cq0-3MMs?usp=sharing</p></details> |
| **[Structure-Aware Skill Retrieval and Arbitration for Optimization Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41909v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) can turn natural-language optimization tasks into solver-checked answers, but current agents remain brittle under narrative rewrites and often store reusable experience at the instance level. We introduce \textsc{ArchOR}, an archetype-centric skill agent that builds on \textsc{OptSkills} by adding two retrieval-focused modules. \emph{Centroid-Aware Representation Learning} (CARL) refines the ordering of cached skill representations through a low-rank prototype residual. \emph{Signature-Guided Retrieval} (S\textsuperscript{3}R) attaches open-dimensional structural signatures to skills, prioritizes candidates by weighted Jaccard overlap, and bounds semantic re-ranking to a top-$K$ set. A baseline-preserving arbitration rule determines when the structural route should replace the original selector. With the same DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash backbone and released skill library, \textsc{ArchOR} reaches 65.00\% Micro-Avg.\ Pass@1 across five benchmarks, improving over \textsc{OptSkills} by 1.64~pp Micro-Avg.\ and 2.68~pp Macro-Avg.</p></details> |  |
| **[Peeling the Matryoshka: Extracting Ontological Structure from Retrieval Embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41785v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Taxonomy construction is essential for knowledge-intensive NLP yet remains costly: clustering-based methods (i) need careful per-corpus tuning and yield unstable hierarchies, while LLM-based pipelines (ii) depend on expensive task-specific training that does not transfer across domains. We report an empirical finding that bears on both: in Matryoshka Representation Learning (MRL) embeddings, where each leading prefix is itself a usable, coarser vector, prefix length aligns with the coarse-to-fine granularity of gold taxonomies; shuffled- and reverse-dimension controls isolate the alignment to the learned early prefix dimensions. The pattern holds across three architecturally distinct MRL models. To test whether this regularity is usable, we introduce PEEL (Peeling Embeddings to Extract Latent ontologies): a minimal probe over these frozen embeddings, trained once with an order-embedding objective that represents hierarchy as vector containment; a minimum spanning arborescence then decodes taxonomic DAGs. Using only a concept vocabulary (no documents), PEEL recovers taxonomic DAGs with high structural fidelity on two OLLM benchmarks, transferring to a new domain with no target-edge supervision. The end-to-end OLLM pipeline, which jointly discovers concepts and induces structure from document corpora, is reported as a cross-task reference. Pre-trained retrieval embeddings encode a decodable granularity gradient; a minimal probe renders it as explicit parent-child (is-a) structure.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to EMNLP 2026 (Main Conference). 13 pages, 6 figures, 7 tables. Code: https://github.com/Eunchan24/PEEL</p></details> |
| **[Towards Universal Tabular Embeddings: A Benchmark Across Data Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.21696v3)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Tabular foundation models aim to learn universal representations of tabular data that transfer across tasks and domains, enabling applications such as table retrieval, semantic search and table-based prediction. To address this, we introduce TEmBed, the Tabular Embedding Test Bed, a unified benchmark for systematically evaluating tabular embeddings across four representation levels: cell, row, column, and table. Evaluating a diverse set of tabular representation learning models, we show that which model to use depends on the task and representation level. | **[MuCALD-SplitFed: Causal-Latent Diffusion for Privacy-Preserving Multi-Task Split-Federated Medical Image Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.04108v2)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Federated Learning enables decentralized training by aggregating model updates across clients without sharing raw data, while Split Federated Learning further partitions the model between clients and a server to reduce computation and communication at the client side. Multi-task FL extends these frameworks by allowing clients to handle different tasks, but often introduces instability and privacy vulnerabilities. This study proposes \textbf{MuCALD-SplitFed}, a multi-task SplitFed framework that integrates causal representation learning and latent diffusion. | **[Evolution of Financial AI: From Statistical Models to Agentic AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41228v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Artificial intelligence (AI) in finance has evolved from statistical learning and machine learning for structured prediction, through deep learning and foundation models for representation learning, language understanding, and reasoning, to retrieval-augmented generation for knowledge-grounded reasoning and agentic AI for autonomous planning and execution. We first show that the major paradigms of financial AI address fundamentally different classes of problems, rather than forming a strict hierarchy of capability. We then review the current state of research on AI in finance and trace how these paradigms have reshaped financial data, financial tasks, decision support, and human-AI collaboration. Building on this, we propose practical guidelines for matching AI paradigms to task characteristics, and show that more advanced models, such as foundation models or autonomous agents, are not universally superior. We conclude by outlining open challenges for researchers and practitioners working toward reliable financial AI.</p></details> |  |
| **[From Prediction to Action: Population Health World Model for Digital Medicine](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24662v2)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Digital medicine now captures data that traces individual health trajectories and reveals population health dynamics through electronic health records, claims, remote monitoring, wearable devices, mobile devices, social sensing data, medical images, etc. But much of its modeling still centers on task-specific, outcome-specific, and prediction-specific estimation. The next frontier is not simply better prediction, but action in an evolving health world shaped by multiple decision-makers, heterogeneous populations, resource constraints, and feedback in care delivery. This is why digital medicine now needs world models: not only to estimate what will happen, but to represent how actions change what can happen next -- whom to prioritize, how to allocate scarce resources, how to adapt care across subgroups, and how interventions reshape future health trajectories and population dynamics. We call such a model population health world model (PHWM): a more foundational representation of an evolving health world in which health states, individual trajectories, population health dynamics, interventions, constraints, and feedback are intrinsically coupled. Building a PHWM endows three unmet structural gaps in current digital medicine: dynamic health-state modeling, population-level intervention intelligence, and validation, equity, and governance for action-oriented use. We argue that addressing these gaps requires three minimal capabilities: representation learning under action-dependent observation, multi-scale intervention intelligence, and validation with explicit decision boundaries, while highlighting the importance of social, contextual, and public-health sensing data for action-oriented digital medicine.</p></details> |  |

| **[Your Spending Needs Attention: Modeling Financial Habits with Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.23267v3)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Predictive models play a crucial role in the financial industry, enabling risk prediction, fraud detection, and personalized recommendations, where slight changes in core model performance can result in billions of dollars in revenue or losses. Exhaustive evaluation of trained binary codes reveals why nearly full use of a memory's information budget can coexist with limited retrieval. In a nested Bell-correlation construction, quantum assistance improves average retrieval over the optimal classical one-bit benchmark within the same communicated budget. These results give representation learning a design principle that connects information capacity to the organization and extraction of target information. By locating the constraints on later answers, the framework can inform memory allocation, encoding objectives, and decoder outputs, providing a basis for seeking better retrieval within a fixed budget.</p></details> | <details><summary>13 pa...</summary><p>13 pages, 3 figures (including Appendix)</p></details> |

## Causal Inference
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[When Everyone Can Build a Model: Mechanistic Inference in the Age of AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41454v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Modern deep learning and AI-assisted code generation are lowering the technical barrier to building mathematical models of biological systems that reproduce complex observations. This lets researchers with domain expertise build semi-mechanistic, hybrid models constrained by prior knowledge and augmented with learned structure without extensive training in modelling. However, easier construction does not make interpretation easier: predictive success of such so-called grey-box models does not establish that an inferred mechanism corresponds to the one that generated the data, and the learned component can absorb errors in the mechanistic part. Many biological modelling problems are underdetermined, so several, possibly infinitely many, models may reproduce the same data equally well. This ambiguity enters at two modelling choices: which variables can affect others, and which response function describes the underlying mechanism. For the first choice, causal inference provides a formal framework stating which causal relationships and intervention effects are identifiable from the data and available knowledge. For the second, ensemble analyses quantify the uncertainty and the range of behaviours still allowed by models that fit equally well. Together, they turn a single good fit into an explicit account of what is supported, what remains open, and which experiment would decide.</p></details> | 8 pages, 2 figures |
| **[Controllable User Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.11519v2)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Using offline datasets to evaluate conversational agents often fails to cover rare scenarios or to support testing new policies. This has motivated the use of controllable user simulators for targeted, counterfactual evaluation, typically implemented by prompting or fine-tuning large language models. In this work, we formalize controllable simulation as a causal inference problem. By bridging natural language evaluation with off-policy evaluation methodology, we show that the standard practice of training simulators via supervised fine-tuning on post-hoc trajectory labels yields a structurally biased model. Specifically, these labels are inextricably coupled to the data-generating behavior policy, injecting a look-ahead bias that breaks causal consistency. Furthermore, we prove that under policy shift this failure causes the variance of evaluation metrics to explode geometrically, a phenomenon we term controllability collapse. To restore causal consistency, we establish theoretical conditions for accurate simulation and propose practical training mitigations: a priori controls, step-wise dynamic controls, and direct policy-conditioned learning. Empirical evaluation confirms that while standard global controls distort conversational distributions and collapse behavioral diversity, our causally grounded simulators eliminate look-ahead bias, preserve natural variance, and exhibit robust zero-shot generalization to unseen agent behaviors.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted Neurips 2026</p></details> |
| **[Scaling Causal Mediation for Complex Systems: A Framework for Root Cause Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.14764v2)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Modern operational systems ranging from logistics and cloud infrastructure to industrial IoT, are governed by complex, interdependent processes. Understanding how interventions propagate through such systems requires causal inference methods that go beyond direct effects to quantify mediated pathways. Traditional mediation analysis, while effective in simple settings, fails to scale to the high-dimensional directed acyclic graphs (DAGs) encountered in practice, particularly when multiple treatments and mediators interact. In this paper, we propose a scalable mediation analysis framework tailored for large causal DAGs involving multiple treatments and mediators. Our approach systematically decomposes total effects into interpretable direct and indirect components. We demonstrate its practical utility through applied case studies in fulfillment center logistics, where complex dependencies and non-controllable factors often obscure root causes.</p></details> | <details><summary>arXiv...</summary><p>arXiv admin note: This submission has been withdrawn because it does not meet arXiv's research content quality standards</p></details> |
| **[Causal Analysis of Author Demographics in Academic Peer Review](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.06641v2)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Academic meritocracy is jeopardized by systematic imbalances; for example, whereas Black and Hispanic individuals constitute over 30% of the U.S. population, they represent fewer than 10% of tenured academics in science and engineering. Our research indicates statistically substantial causal disadvantages for authors from minority racial groups (average treatment effects [ATE]: -0.42 points in ranking), female authors (ATE: -0.25), and those associated with institutions in the Global South (ATE: -0.57). The exhibited biases emphasize the pressing necessity for fairness interventions in both conventional and AI-based review processes, indicating that such measures are essential for establishing a more equitable and credible scientific environment.</p></details> | <details><summary>arXiv...</summary><p>arXiv admin note: This submission has been withdrawn because it does not meet arXiv's research content quality standards</p></details> |
| **[Differentially Private Covariate Balancing Causal Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.14789v3)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Differential privacy is the leading mathematical framework for privacy protection, providing a probabilistic guarantee that safeguards individuals' private information when publishing statistics from a dataset. This guarantee is achieved by applying a randomized algorithm to the original data, which introduces unique challenges in data analysis by distorting inherent patterns. In particular, causal inference using observational data while preserving privacy is challenging because it requires a good covariate balance between treatment groups, but checking the covariate balance is challenging when privacy is a primary concern because releasing any statistic (e.g., t-statistic) for the balance check compromises privacy. Additionally, the performance of the privatized estimator critically depends on the choice of differential privacy mechanisms, and it remains unexplored how privacy-protecting causal inference could be conducted with observational data. In this article, we present a differentially private two-stage covariate balancing weighting estimator to infer causal effects from observational data. Our algorithm produces both point and interval estimators with statistical guarantees, such as covariate balance, rate optimality, and oracle budget allocation, under a given privacy budget.</p></details> | 47 pages |

Under a model imposing only minimal regularity conditions, the target is Hellinger discontinuous when both inverse problems are ill-posed: arbitrarily small changes in the data-generating law can produce arbitrarily large changes in the target, ruling out informative locally uniform inference. Imposing a uniform bound on a minimum-norm solution restores Hellinger continuity under regularity conditions, but does not by itself ensure stable root-n inference. If, in addition, both inverse problems are ill-posed and the solution whose norm is uniformly bounded is nonunique at a law $P_0$, then under regularity conditions the target fails to be pathwise differentiable; consequently, asymptotically linear estimators exhibit nonvanishing root-$n$ bias under some local alternatives, causing Wald confidence intervals to undercover asymptotically. | **[Tracing the Influence of Retrieved Literature on Scientific Ideation in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39060v2)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to generate research ideas from scientific literature. Existing systems combine literature retrieval with search, generation, and refinement, making it difficult to determine how the retrieval strategy itself shapes the ideas generated. We isolate this contribution by comparing five retrieval strategies and a No Literature baseline under a shared generation protocol, using 45,000 ideas across 15 machine learning topics and ten open-weight models. Relative to no literature basline, all five retrieval strategies reduce diversity among ideas generated from the same set of papers by $5.6\%$--$17.9\%$, while most increase diversity by $10.2\%$--$18.1\%$ when ideas from different sets are pooled. Effects on idea quality and novelty are modest on average but vary across topics. We further apply a JSD-based attribution method to trace the influence of individual input papers on each idea's motivation, core idea, approach, and validation. In $79.3\%$ of ideas, only one or two distinct papers receive the highest attribution scores across these four parts. These leading sources often recur across ideas generated from the same set of papers, and groups with more varied source attribution contain more diverse ideas ($r=0.70$). These findings motivate various sets of literature and the use of attribution to guide the choice of sources for subsequent ideas.</p></details> | <details><summary>Updat...</summary><p>Updated preprint. Under review</p></details> |
| **[Learning to Reason from Reference Samples Alone: Reinforced Adversarial Learning for Modern LLM Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38851v2)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In many settings where LLM post-training is desirable, high-quality answers are recognizable but difficult to score with explicit rewards. We ask whether a co-trained LLM judge can turn reference answers alone into the sole reward for on-policy post-training of reasoning LLMs. We call this method Reinforced Adversarial Learning (RAL). We first validate RAL on three verifiable tasks (integer multiplication, code-based arithmetic-expression synthesis, and SMILES-to-formula translation), using the verifiers only for evaluation. With sufficiently capable judges and enough training, RAL matches direct RLVR. We then apply RAL to scientific abstract generation in two settings. In the first setting, RAL guides the generator to produce abstracts in the scientific domain of the reference abstracts, even though the prompt leaves that domain unspecified. In the second setting, we generate abstracts for U.S. National Institutes of Health grant proposals conditioned on the grant title. Human scientists and an ensemble of LLM judges find our generations harder to distinguish from real abstracts than those of frontier-LLM baselines. These results suggest a path toward post-training reasoning models in scientific and other high-value domains, where increasingly capable judges infer hard-to-specify reward functions from example answers.</p></details> | <details><summary>31 pa...</summary><p>31 pages, 6 figures, 10 tables, including references and appendices</p></details> |

ACCT transforms the KV cache produced by a source model into sparse KV states that can be directly consumed by a target model, thereby enabling cross-model reuse of context computation and decoupling context computation from downstream inference. ACCT consists of three lightweight components: the Cartographer identifies query-relevant context tokens for each target layer and KV head; the Allocator determines how many context tokens each target layer and KV head retains under a shared total KV budget; and the Compiler transforms selected source KV into sparse target KV. The target model can therefore perform inference using the transferred KV without reprocessing the original context. We evaluate ACCT in two complementary settings comprising five general-capability benchmarks and six highly context-dependent tasks. Experiments show that ACCT matches or exceeds the target model's original inference performance even when reducing the KV cache size by 75%. At a context length of approximately 19K tokens, ACCT's KV conversion achieves a 6.5-21.2 times speedup over target context prefill. | **[Same Question, Different Answers: Auditing Persona-Dependent Variation in Six LLMs on Smoking-Cessation Advice](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42039v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Hundreds of millions of people now ask large language model (LLM) chatbots for health advice, which raises a question that is simple to pose and hard to answer: does everyone get the same advice? What the models say depends on who appears to be asking. Every model named FDA-approved prescription cessation medications less often to teen personas than to adult personas, by 32 to 67 percentage points, and in five of six models a persona presenting health- care expertise received fewer facts than a persona presenting none. We then evaluate one deployable intervention: a short system prompt specifying a factual floor, the set of core med- ical facts every user should receive when they are relevant, while leaving tone, vocabulary, and framing free to adapt. The prompt narrows persona differences in every model, but its effect on overall fact coverage varies in both magnitude and direction across models, so a prompt validated on one system cannot be assumed to transfer to another. Two cautions frame these results. Equal mention rates are not always the correct clinical target, because prescription cessation pharmacother- apy is not recommended for minors, so a defensible factual floor must be age-aware rather than uniform. And because our personas vary on several attributes at once, we report scenario- level differences rather than isolated effects of age or expertise. These proxies are unreliable: disabled thinking modes may still emit reasoning, while long traces may contain filler rather than genuine inference. We evaluate six prompting interventions on six LLMs across Boolean, multiple-choice, and open-ended questions. We find that explicit no-think controls cannot reliably eliminate visible inference. Models instead exhibit "Thinking Inertia": explicit inference persists even under strict controls and becomes more prevalent as the answer space opens. Accuracy remains stable on Boolean and multiple-choice tasks, whereas open-ended tasks reveal a trade-off between answer-only compliance and task accuracy. Rewriting the same questions across answer spaces shows that supplying candidate answers makes answer-only responses easier to produce. These findings establish no-thinking as a non-trivial capability: stopping explicit reasoning cannot be assumed from model settings or instructions alone and deserves systematic evaluation alongside reasoning ability.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by NeurIPS 2026. Across six math benchmarks, SRaR improves average accuracy over RaR by 3.57 and 2.75 points on Qwen3-8B-Non-Thinking and Qwen3-32B-Non-Thinking, respectively. On AIME 2025, it increases the Faithful Reasoning Rate from 34.5% to 46.7% and reduces self-correction looping from 48.1% to 26.5%. SRaR also achieves the best results on three of four code-generation benchmarks.</p></details> | <details><summary>NeurI...</summary><p>NeurIPS 2026; Code available at https://github.com/akarinmoe/SRaR</p></details> |
| **[Instrument-Anchored LLM Judges Transfer Expert Counseling-Quality Ratings Across Three German Corpora](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08055v2)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Automatic assessment of communication quality in counseling conversations is bottlenecked by data: expert-rated corpora are small and expensive to grow. Trained on the other two corpora, this stack predicts the held-out corpus at Spearman $ρ= .533$; in-domain training on the same sessions falls about a fifth short ($.420$; paired gap $+.113$, 95% CI $[+.067, +.157]$), and the gap holds at matched training size and on identical test sessions. The signal lies in what is said: the four judges alone transfer at $.523$, and the nonverbal block adds $+.01$ (CI includes zero). Asking a judge for instrument constructs instead of generic dialogue qualities raises its transfer by about 30% ($.32$ to $.42$); restoring speaker attribution where per-speaker audio was lost is worth $+.08$ to $+.10$ on that fold. | **[ReX-GG: a LLM Ensemble Pipeline for Relation-extraction and Graph Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41901v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Current LLM ensemble frameworks focus on multi-step setups with additional modules for answer ranking, often opting for token and span analysis rather than structured outputs, leading to heavyweight architectures with potential fail states along the pipeline. | **[Automated Keyword Generation Using LLMs in a High-Volume Data Virtualization Service](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41893v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In modern data infrastructure, a data virtualization service (DVS) allows users to create many versions of source datasets for different processing tasks. Data selection, participation, integration, and preprocessing in different tasks result in many virtual datasets [Khan et al., SSC 2026]. To enable an effective search engine in a DVS, it is desirable to automatically generate keywords for each virtual dataset. We conducted experiments with three LLM generators and two embedding techniques. The experiments show that the performances of the five strategies depend on the quality of the text descriptions. | **[No More Import Errors: Constrained Decoding for Guaranteed Valid Imports in LLM-Generated Tests](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41878v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>One of the most common failure reasons of LLM-based test generation pipelines is import errors. We implement CBDL for Python and Java and evaluate it on two test generation tasks, showing that generated tests have 100% valid imports, outperforming both static (RAG and LIBRO) and dynamic (Agentic Repair) import-validity approaches that leave 45%, 10%, and 43% of import errors unresolved. CBDL converts on average 14% of initial import errors into functionally correct tests, increasing overall test correctness by 1-4 percentage points over unconstrained generation. Even when import validity does not directly lead to a correct test, it allows agentic repair pipelines to allocate the repair budget to remaining errors, increasing correct tests by 2-7 percentage points for Python and 1-6 for Java. Finally, we show that a hybrid pipeline, where a small local model equipped with CBDL generates imports while the frontier GPT-5.2 model generates the test body, outperforms generating both with GPT-5.2 alone by 1-3 percentage points. | **[LLM Recommenders Produce Demographic Inequality Through Content Preferences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41854v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly being integrated into systems that rank and recommend online content. We find that demographic inequality emerges from text alone. Models consistently favour longer, more negative, and more opinionated posts, particularly those concerning news and social issues, producing systematic differences in the demographic composition of recommended authors. Recommendation objectives mainly affect the magnitude of these patterns: controversial framing intensifies both content and demographic skew, whereas popularity-oriented framing dampens it, although for some attributes - most notably age - the objective can also reverse which group is favoured. | **[What Standard Evaluations Hide About Detectors of Hijacked LLM Agents: Rare Attacks, Unseen Tools and Benchmark Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41803v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Detectors can flag hijacked large language model (LLM) agents from the traces of the tools they call. We ask what such evaluations hide. On the AgentDrift and AgentDojo benchmarks we test trained detectors that read tool identities, behavior, call sequences, step embeddings or text, and a released LLM action monitor, AlignmentCheck, with thresholds fixed on training data or set by the monitor's released decision rule. First, at a 2% attack rate a detector with an area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUROC) of 0.879 reaches a precision of 0.076, and the mix of negatives alone moves that precision between 0.039 and 0.117. Second, as tool coverage, the share of test tool calls whose tool was seen in training, falls, detector rankings cross near 50% coverage and reverse below it, because unseen tools map to inputs that training never reached. Dropout on tool inputs largely removes the reversal. Third, no detector trained on AgentDrift ranks AgentDojo traces better than chance (AUROC 0.47 to 0.52), including one that reaches 0.996 within AgentDojo. The monitor, trained on neither, reaches 0.930, but with its open backbone its released decision rule would still mostly raise false alerts at a 2% attack rate (precision 0.096). Code, data and every monitor call: https://github.com/Mahmoud-Abbasi-svg/agent-trace-detector-eval</p></details> |
| **[Adapting LLM-SC to Living Sacred Heritage: Domain-Specific Extraction, Semantic Networks and Human Evaluation on Pashupatinath Temple Reviews](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35685v2)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) pipelines can turn visitor reviews into structured data, but generic schemes may miss rituals, sacred spaces, cultural restrictions and emotionally mixed experiences at living sacred heritage sites. | **[Do LLMs Know When They Are Wrong About Molecules? Combining uncertainty and prediction, on lipophilicity the most certain half of the best LLM is the most accurate at small context sizes; on BACE and antiviral, and on lipophilicity at large context sizes, the best LLMs are on par with the best baseline. | **[Precision--Recall Trade-off on the Inference Level for LLMs in Grammatical Error Correction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41698v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) have recently been adopted for Grammatical Error Correction (GEC), achieving strong performance via both full fine-tuning and prompt-based approaches. | **[Supervised and LLM-Augmented Cardinality Estimation for Similarity Queries on Knowledge Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41669v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Over the past decades, query cardinality estimation on relational databases has been proven crucial, enabling the result size estimation of all sub-plans of each query. In this work, we present a novel approach for supervised similarity-aware cardinality estimation on knowledge graphs that incorporates similarities into the model training process, thus treating them as first-class citizens. To this end, we present SCE-KG, a model that employs a novel encoding scheme for cardinality estimation, named SGsplit. We further extend our approach by leveraging a Large Language Model (LLM) to generate training queries that resemble realistic user workloads, and show that combining LLM-generated and random walk samples consistently improves estimation accuracy on test workloads generated by two independent LLMs. Our experimental evaluation reveals that our approach outperforms the state-of-the-art techniques in terms of prediction error, achieving 40x and 37x improvement for star and chain queries, respectively (95th percentile), whereas its online overhead accounts for less than 0.05% of the average query execution time and less than 1% of the average query planning time, showing a sub-millisecond latency in all cases.</p></details> |  |

| **[When Steering Shifts Discourse: Identifying and Intervening on Discourse-Level Changes due to Activation Steering in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41661v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Activation steering is an inference-time technique used to control or modify the behavior, tone, or safety properties of a large language model (LLM) without retraining the model's weights or editing its input prompt, but simply by adding a vector to its residual-stream activations. In this work, we show that steering systematically produces significant changes in discourse dimensions of the generated text, such as its formality, use of evidence, and ambiguity. We present a way of countering this by adding a second, complementary vector that targets the affected discourse dimension. | **[Prompt Less, Infer More: Static Analysis-Guided LLM Type Annotation for Python](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41617v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Type annotations improve the maintainability and reliability of Python programs, but many real-world projects remain under-annotated because manual annotation is time-consuming and error-prone. Recent neural and LLM-based type inference systems can recover missing annotations, yet they often trade accuracy for efficiency. Based on KA, we propose Knowledge Association Reinforcement Unlearning (KARU), a unified reinforcement learning framework that suppresses forgotten knowledge associations while preserving retained ones. Specifically, KARU derives complementary mechanisms from the KA-based formulation: token-level reward construction for finer-grained forgetting supervision, distribution-level regularization for precise knowledge suppression, and proximity-aware retain sampling to mitigate collateral degradation.
