| **[VerifyMAS: Hypothesis Verification for Failure Attribution in LLM-based Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17467v3)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model-driven multi-agent systems (LLM-MAS) excel at complex tasks, yet unreliable agents remain a key bottleneck to system-level reliability. | **[ORION: An Outcome-Driven LLM Advisor for RL-Based Multi-Domain 6G Slice Placement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41563v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Deploying 6G network slices across administrative domains requires an online decision for each arriving request: which domain hosts each virtual network function, how traffic crosses domain boundaries, and whether the slice is admitted. | **[Rimuru: Unified Memory Management for Modern LLM Serving via Elastic Expert Offloading](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40305v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Modern LLMs strain device memory through large model weights and attention states, particularly in resource-constrained scenarios. We propose semantic contract-guided edge potential scheduling (STEPS), a natural language-driven framework that introduces semantic contracts as executable interfaces between user-side semantics and edge-side decisions. Confidence calibration and interpretable mapping rules convert these outputs into preference weights, fulfillment bounds, and semantic uncertainty. STEPS formulates intended per-slot edge scheduling as an exact potential game for execution-node selection, computing-resource provisioning, and bandwidth allocation, followed by capacity-feasible resource realization. Fulfillment-driven feedback signals enable adaptive scheduling under evolving service and network conditions. We establish the existence of pure-strategy Nash equilibrium, prove finite-step convergence of uncapped strict best-response dynamics, and characterize the boundedness of projected adaptive-control parameters. Experiments under synthetic and Melbourne-based hybrid settings demonstrate improved contract fulfillment and reduced service loss relative to several adapted benchmarks. Post-drift evaluations further show that feedback adaptation improves fulfillment and admission coverage under the tested conditions.</p></details> |  |
| **[Efficient LLM-based Subgraph Retrieval for Multi-Hop Knowledge Base Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39667v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-hop Knowledge Base Question Answering (KBQA) aims to find answer entities in the knowledge base that are multiple hops away from the entities in the question. Information retrieval-based (IR-based) methods extract a pivotal subgraph from the entire KB to locate candidate answers and then evaluate their plausibility through semantic matching with the question. However, we observed that the extracted subgraphs often include nodes that are weakly related or irrelevant to the question. Without a proper node filtering mechanism, the number of irrelevant nodes grows as the number of hops increases, leading to excessive consumption of computational resources. To address these challenges, this study introduces an efficient LLM-based subgraph retrieval method for multi-hop knowledge base question answering, M-ER. The framework leverages Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) to transform subgraph exploration into a tree-structured search process. During the MCTS selection phase, nodes that are highly relevant to the question are prioritized for inclusion in the subgraph, eliminating the need to traverse all nodes in the KB. The framework further incorporates a large language model (LLM) to refine the search direction, ensuring that exploration remains focused on nodes relevant to the question. In addition, selected nodes are quantitatively scored, and these scores are fed back into the node selection process to effectively filter out irrelevant candidates, thereby improving the quality of the subgraph. This mechanism not only narrows the search space but also enhances the overall efficiency of multi-hop KBQA. Experiments on the WebQSP benchmark demonstrate that M-ER achieves 78.88\% on the Hits@1 metric, while also improving computational efficiency. | **[Reliability of LLM Judges for Evaluating Entity Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09554v2)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Entity Alignment (EA) identifies equivalent entities across knowledge graphs and is critical for knowledge base integration and ontology merging. Judge discrimination collapses under label-exposing prompts (J-ROC-AUC 0.12--0.87), while a label-free protocol recovers near-ceiling capability on distinctive-name datasets (0.98--1.00 for NeuSymEA) and significant recovery on biomedical pairs (0.93--0.95). Counterfactual label flips show that the visible label causally shifts scores by more than 2 points in 53--99% of pairs (FSR), with sensitivity growing with model capability, a pattern we call the frontier model paradox: stronger judges are more label-sensitive, not less, yet also better label-free predictors. The direction of the shifts is mixed, so we report this as an observed pattern. A blinded two-annotator human evaluation (102 pairs, Cohen's $κ$ = 0.902) provides a gold-label reference. We release the first biomedical EA benchmark (MeSH--SNOMED CT, 15K pairs) and a reproducible auditing framework for LLM judge reliability in EA. Code and data are available at https://github.com/vaibhavalakshmiravideshik/llm-as-a-judge-entity-alignment.</p></details> |  |
| **[Sticky Collusion: Persistence of LLM Agent Collusion after Incentive Removal and Partner Replacement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39660v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We ask whether collusion between AI agents survives once formed, when its incentive is removed or a colluder is replaced. We save each pair's history once its collusion has formed and resume it under interventions, with three models (GPT-6-Luna, DeepSeek V4.1 Flash and Gemini-3.1-Flash-Lite), \TotalHistories{} saved histories and \TotalRuns{} runs. Collusion is \emph{sticky}. First, it outlives its incentive: told that the reward has stopped, pairs still accept each other's unverified work in \GptToldRate, \DsToldRate{} and \GemToldRate{} of episodes, including pairs whose collusion began with an explicit agreement, and a payoff penalty has no significant effect. Second, replacing a colluder ends the pair's collusion but not the colluder's behaviour. Without the reward, the remaining colluder still accepts unverified work in \GptSwapSilentColluderRate, \DsSwapSilentColluderRate{} and \GemSwapSilentColluderRate{} of its verdicts while the newcomer never does; with the reward, collusion re-forms around it, even when the newcomer is a different model. Third, the agents' memory carries it: wiping memory ends it in every history, but partial memory often sustains it. Fourth, removing its cause ends collusion that has already formed: once the channel is repaired, colluders reject planted faulty work as reliably as fresh agents, immediately and with no monitor needed to catch it first.</p></details> |  |

| **[RoboArchitect: Automating the Design of Robotic Agents with Persistent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42141v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent work shows that a frozen robot policy performs far better inside an agent harness, where control code decides when to query the policy, consult vision tools, or command the arm directly. Yet today these harnesses are built by hand and redesigned for each new robot or benchmark. We show that harness design can be automated, offering a strong alternative to finetuning on a novel setup. In RoboArchitect, a coding model observes a robot's rollouts, diagnoses failures, and iteratively revises the harness around the frozen policy. The harness evolves on a set of episodes for its optimization and is evaluated on held-out scenes and tasks. Without updating any model weights, RoboArchitect raises average success from 9.7% to 43.3% on unseen scenes (4.5x) and from 12.7% to 40.2% on unseen tasks (3.2x), across multiple benchmarks and policies. The gains are largest where the benchmark is far from the policy's training data: wrapping a general-purpose policy, the evolved harness yields a specialist that often outperforms even the finetuned version. Intuitive control strategies are automatically designed, such as lifting a jammed arm clear or reaching in horizontally when an obstacle blocks the way from above. The harness also evolves its own persistent memory, carrying learned experience, such as effective grasp depths, into later decisions. These results suggest that substantial headroom in robotic manipulation lies in the agent harness, and its design can be effectively automated and optimized without weight updates.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project Website: https://roboarchitect-ai.github.io/</p></details> |
| **[Metacognitive Decoding: Efficient Inference for Diffusion Language Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37642v2)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Diffusion language models offer a promising path to efficient agents through parallel token generation. However, efficient interaction requires adapting both where computation is spent and how much text is generated. We introduce a metacognitive controller that guides diffusion generation according to the needs of the current response and the agent's interaction with the environment. It learns when to finalize tokens and how far generation should extend, reducing overall computation within responses. Our controller, a lightweight plug-in for the diffusion backbone, is trained on agent trajectories to balance correctness and generation cost. This approach connects the agent's generation process to the interaction the agent serves. Experiments across five benchmarks demonstrate that our approach reduces inference FLOPs by 20.6% on average compared with the most efficient baseline, while maintaining competitive task accuracy. Ablation studies further validate the effectiveness of our approach.</p></details> | 10 pages, 4 figures |
| **[LazyAgent: Demand-Driven Materialization and Physical Optimization of Agentic Programs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23058v2)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agent runtimes that plan before acting run every step as soon as it is ready. Neither these runtimes nor their benchmarks distinguish a step that is ready from a step the request needs, and the difference is large: across 303 requests on 154 production workflows, an eager runtime that stops once the goal is met spends 62.2% of its CPU on work the request never needed. LazyAgent is, to our knowledge, the first complete formulation of demand-driven execution for agent runtimes. Its rule: run a ready step only if the current request needs it, and keep that answer exact as the plan changes. A demanded set derived from the program decides, in one traversal with no model calls, so LazyAgent spends nothing outside the request. Because demand is defined exactly, its behavior is proved: builders know before any run where demand must save and where it must tie. The bill is known before a plan runs, so LazyAgent can act as a query optimizer to choose among candidate workflows. Measured live against that eager runtime, LazyAgent saves 51.7% and 52.1% of container time on release gates where a frontier LLM writes the patches, 12.8% of tokens on DataSciBench plans at matched quality, and 24.0% of dollars inside a live LangGraph application, with no losses. When the LLM re-plans after real code failures, it solves every solvable goal with 11.7% fewer tokens per goal. On the production workflows it saves 42.0% of CPU, ties exactly where a request needs the whole graph, and adds at most 0.43% overhead. The same set steers every physical operation: projection saves 80-89% of a shared CI step, where the identical eager control saves 0.0%. Public agent benchmarks are designed the same way as eager runtimes, so they cannot show this difference. We compose our test programs from their tasks and call on industry to build benchmarks that measure how efficiently agent workflows run.</p></details> | <details><summary>57 pa...</summary><p>57 pages. v2: rewrote the abstract and introduction; added a remark on pricing plans to the appendix; results unchanged</p></details> |
| **[The Envelope Layer: An Underestimated Surface of LLM Agent Defense](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37415v2)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Prior work framed the envelope layer as the channel through which malicious content enters an agent, but did not examine the effects of label strength, envelope authenticity, scaffolds, or existing memories. Following its GDA Measurement and A/B/C/D adjudication, this paper takes the A2A-TIBA attack as the test vehicle and the semantic breach rate as the primary indicator, and characterizes the security boundary of the envelope layer through a 2x2 factorial experiment, a memory experiment, and control experiments (an unlabeled same-protocol baseline, a one-day interleaved control, and benign tasks). The results show that, under authentic envelopes, strong labels consistently reduce the semantic breach rate (-56 to -61 percentage points), and the benefit of weak labels is about a quarter to a half of that of strong labels; forging the envelope weakens the strong label's benefit (interaction OR = 2.10, p = 0.022) and its direction varies across frontends; the effect of memory valence exceeds that of any label manipulation; success memory raises the semantic breach rate and failure memory lowers it, while most conflict combinations return to the no-memory baseline; the interleaved control reproduces the direction that "unlabeled is worst"; and on benign tasks the cost of strong labels appears in layers across frontends and backends. Accordingly, this paper extends the envelope layer into "admission surface plus context assembly surface," whose security boundary is a dynamic outcome jointly shaped by label claims, frontend permissions, and historical memory.</p></details> |  |
| **[RAMA: Retrieval-Augmented Multi-Agent Routing with Episodic Memory for LEO Satellite Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42068v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Low-Earth orbit (LEO) satellite networks present a challenging routing environment due to rapidly changing topology, traffic, and link conditions. We propose RAMA, a retrieval-augmented multi-agent DRL framework with Approximate Information State (AIS), in which each satellite combines its local observation, recent routing history, neighboring state information, and retrieved entries from a per-satellite episodic memory. Retrieved experiences are integrated through a Transformer-based decision network trained with AIS-based auxiliary objectives. With weights frozen in a 140-satellite Kepler constellation, RAMA reduces mean end-to-end latency by 13.4% and 99th-percentile latency by 66.7% relative to the state-of-theart double deep Q-network (DDQN) routing method, mainly by avoiding long detours. | **[REA: A Routed Embodied Agent Runtime for Asynchronous Brain-Cerebellum Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42053v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Foundation models are becoming remarkably reliable at high-level reasoning, yet they remain too slow and costly to serve as fine-grained controllers. For example, an agent that calls the VLA in the agentic loop can stall the fast execution, while a VLA that calls the agent on errors tends to re-trigger fallbacks repeatedly or completely miss silent failures. To address this, we propose REA (Routed Embodied Agent), a unified runtime where a deliberative System 2, an embodied router System 1 and reactive System 0 policies cooperate under a single golden rule - no running policy waits on the brain. Instead of querying the brain at each failure, REA employs a route memory that stores each policy's measured success ahead of time, a boundary-triggered judge gate that consults the brain on policy execution, and a shared-budget escalation ladder for bounded recovery. Evaluated on 3,000 test episodes across 60 LIBERO-Pro tasks, REA reaches 95.9% success and beats the best single-agent baseline in its own policy pool (94.7%) while reducing control steps by 25% and control time by 17%. REA also transfers to BEHAVIOR-1K and a real Unitree G1 humanoid, achieving the highest observed success among the compared methods.</p></details> | 32 pages, 14 figures |
| **[MIRAGE: Auditing Anti-Muslim Bias in Frontier LLMs Across Reasoning, Agentic, and Time-Coupled Conditions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.16562v2)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Five years after the discovery of persistent anti-Muslim bias in large language models, most evaluations remain confined to single-turn prompt completion, a setting that no longer reflects how frontier LLMs are deployed. Second, as tool coverage, the share of test tool calls whose tool was seen in training, falls, detector rankings cross near 50% coverage and reverse below it, because unseen tools map to inputs that training never reached. Dropout on tool inputs largely removes the reversal. Third, no detector trained on AgentDrift ranks AgentDojo traces better than chance (AUROC 0.47 to 0.52), including one that reaches 0.996 within AgentDojo. Beyond Gaussian models, we show that two independent nonnegative observations suffice to elicit any finite mean above a positive null threshold, without any further distributional knowledge, but one observation cannot combine strict mean elicitation with individual admissibility on this class. On 62 calibrated systematic failures, the best of 13 frontier models (Grok 4.7) reaches a causal repair of +51.3 points; the top eight models are statistically indistinguishable, the flagship models of the two largest vendors rank 8th and 12th, and 5 items are repaired by no model. Forcing a strong model to intervene at the last step costs it 17.1 points, so the last-step shortcut of annotation-based attribution benchmarks does not transfer. On 87 runs, the two best live supervisors are statistically tied and one of them is a small open-weight model (DeepSeek V4.1 Flash); the supervisor that is among the best post-hoc diagnosers (GPT-6.1 Sol) is the weakest live one (-14.5 points on the same runs, p = 0.002), so post-hoc diagnostic performance can be a poor proxy for live oversight. | **[Events as Triggers for Behavioral Diversity in Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.12388v3)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Effective multi-agent cooperation requires agents to adopt diverse behaviors as task conditions evolve-and to do so at the right moment. | **[Towards Elastic Least Privilege for Agent Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40981v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>General-purpose AI agents require authority that changes between tasks and during execution. Costs are visible to the planner, which can prefer lower-privilege choices and release authority it no longer needs. Authority returns automatically to the baseline when a lease expires, when it is released, or when the budget is exhausted, while persistent legitimate demand may prompt an owner-approved change to the baseline. | **[Renegotiable Options Fail to Prevent Hold-Up Between Two Profit-Maximizing Frontier LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40961v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Do contract terms that protect investment between people also protect it between profit-maximizing language-model agents? When we changed the numbers so that the buyer would exercise the option even without adaptation, adapting only added cost, and no seller adapted: the agents did not simply adapt whenever an option was offered or whenever its price covered the cost. | **[Agentic World Modeling for Agent Test-time Scaling without Environment Replica](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40927v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Test-time scaling (TTS) improves large language model agents by sampling and selecting among multiple candidate trajectories, but common realizations assume that these trajectories can be executed in parallel. | **[STRIATUM-CTF: A Protocol-Driven Agentic Framework for Autonomous CTF Solving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.22577v2)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Autonomous cyber operations increasingly depend on agents that can chain reconnaissance, exploitation, and analysis across many tools while maintaining state over long task horizons.
